                "question": "Consider the following statements:\n\nI. Carbon dioxide (CO₂) emissions in India are less than 0.5 t CO₂/capita.\nII. In terms of CO₂ emissions from fuel combustion, India ranks second in Asia-Pacific region.\nIII. Electricity and heat producers are the largest source of CO₂ emissions in India.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "Let's check each statement about India's carbon emissions:\n\n(I) 'CO₂ emissions in India are less than 0.5 t CO₂/capita' — **INCORRECT**. India's per capita CO₂ emissions are approximately 1.9-2.0 tonnes per year (as of recent data). While this is much lower than countries like the US (~15 t/capita) or China (~8 t/capita), it is still far above 0.5 tonnes. The statement significantly understates India's per capita emissions. ✗\n\n(II) 'India ranks second in Asia-Pacific in CO₂ emissions from fuel combustion' — **CORRECT**. China is the largest emitter in the Asia-Pacific region (and globally), and India is the second largest. India surpassed Japan to take this position. Overall globally, India is the third-largest CO₂ emitter after China and the US. ✓\n\n(III) 'Electricity and heat producers are the largest source of CO₂ emissions in India' — **CORRECT**. India's power sector (electricity generation and heat production) is the single largest source of CO₂ emissions, primarily because India still relies heavily on coal-fired thermal power plants for about 70% of its electricity generation. ✓\n\nStatements II and III are correct. **Answer is (c).**",

                "question": "Regarding Peacock tarantula (Gooty tarantula), consider the following statements:\n\nI. It is an omnivorous crustacean.\nII. Its natural habitat in India is only limited to some forest areas.\nIII. In its natural habitat, it is an arboreal species.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "Let's evaluate each statement about the Peacock tarantula (Gooty tarantula, scientific name: Poecilothrus metallica):\n\n(I) 'It is an omnivorous crustacean' — **INCORRECT** on two counts. First, a tarantula is an arachnid (spider), NOT a crustacean. Crustaceans include crabs, shrimp, and lobsters — a completely different class of arthropods. Second, tarantulas are carnivores/insectivores — they eat insects, small lizards, and other small animals. They are NOT omnivores. ✗ (Two mistakes in one statement!)\n\n(II) 'Its natural habitat in India is limited to some forest areas' — **CORRECT**. The Gooty tarantula is found only in a very small area of deciduous forest near Giddalur and Nandyal in Andhra Pradesh. It has an extremely restricted range, which is why it is classified as Critically Endangered on the IUCN Red List. ✓\n\n(III) 'In its natural habitat, it is an arboreal species' — **CORRECT**. The Gooty tarantula lives in trees (arboreal), making its home in holes and crevices in tall trees. This is unusual because many tarantula species are ground-dwelling. Its striking metallic blue coloration makes it one of the most visually spectacular spiders in the world. ✓\n\nStatements II and III are correct. **Answer is (d).**",
                "option_a": "I only",
                "option_b": "I and III",
                "option_c": "II only",
                "option_d": "II and III",
                "pyq_year": "2025",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Let's evaluate each statement about the *Peacock tarantula* (*Gooty tarantula*, scientific name: *Poecilotheria metallica*):  \n\n(I) 'It is an omnivorous crustacean' — **INCORRECT** on two counts.  \n\nFirst, a tarantula is an *arachnid* (spider), NOT a *crustacean*.  \n\nCrustaceans include *crabs*, *shrimp*, and *lobsters* — a completely different class of *arthropods*.  \n\nSecond, tarantulas are *carnivores/insectivores* — they eat *insects*, *small lizards*, and other *small animals*.  \n\nThey are NOT omnivores. ✗ (Two mistakes in one statement!)  \n\n(II) 'Its natural habitat in India is limited to some forest areas' — **CORRECT**.  \n\nThe *Gooty tarantula* is found only in a very small area of *deciduous forest* near *Giddalur* and *Nandyal* in *Andhra Pradesh*.  \n\nIt has an extremely restricted range, which is why it is classified as **Critically Endangered** on the *IUCN Red List*. ✓  \n\n(III) 'In its natural habitat, it is an arboreal species' — **CORRECT**.  \n\nThe *Gooty tarantula* lives in *trees* (arboreal), making its home in *holes* and *crevices* in tall trees.  \n\nThis is unusual because many tarantula species are *ground-dwelling*.  \n\nIts striking *metallic blue coloration* makes it one of the most visually spectacular spiders in the world. ✓  \n\nStatements II and III are correct. **Answer is (d).**  "

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "Statement I: 'Brazil produces more ethanol than the USA' — **INCORRECT**. The United States is the world's LARGEST ethanol producer, followed by Brazil. The US produces roughly 55-60% of global ethanol, while Brazil produces about 25-30%. So Brazil does NOT produce more ethanol than the US. ✗\n\nStatement II: 'US uses corn, Brazil uses sugarcane for ethanol' — **CORRECT**. This is a key difference in global ethanol production. The US primarily uses corn (maize) as feedstock — corn is fermented to produce ethanol, which is blended with gasoline (E10, E15, E85 blends). Brazil primarily uses sugarcane — the sugar is extracted and fermented, and sugarcane ethanol is used extensively in Brazilian vehicles (many run on 100% ethanol or flex-fuel). Interestingly, sugarcane-based ethanol is more energy-efficient (produces about 8 units of energy for every 1 unit used in production) compared to corn-based ethanol (about 1.6 units output per 1 unit input). ✓\n\nStatement I is not correct, Statement II is correct. **Answer is (d).**",

                "question": "The World Bank warned that India could become one of the first places where wet-bulb temperatures routinely exceed 35°C. Which of the following statements best reflect(s) the implication of the above-said report?\n\nI. Peninsular India will most likely suffer from flooding, tropical cyclones and droughts.\nII. The survival of animals including humans will be affected as shedding of their body heat through perspiration becomes difficult.\n\nSelect the correct answer using the code given below.",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "The wet-bulb temperature is a measure that combines heat AND humidity. At a wet-bulb temperature of 35°C, the human body can no longer cool itself through sweat evaporation because the air is too saturated with moisture — this makes it potentially lethal for humans and animals.\n\n(I) 'Peninsular India will suffer from flooding, tropical cyclones and droughts' — **CORRECT**. The same warming patterns that cause extreme wet-bulb temperatures are associated with broader climate disruptions in peninsular India: intensified monsoons leading to flooding, warmer sea surface temperatures fueling more powerful cyclones, and irregular rainfall patterns causing droughts in some regions. These are all documented implications of the warming trend that the World Bank report highlights. ✓\n\n(II) 'Survival of animals including humans affected as perspiration becomes difficult' — **CORRECT**. This is the core danger of wet-bulb temperatures exceeding 35°C. At this threshold, even a healthy person resting in shade with unlimited water will die within hours because sweat cannot evaporate effectively when humidity is too high. The body continues to produce heat through metabolism but cannot shed it, leading to hyperthermia and organ failure. This affects all mammals and many other animals that rely on evaporative cooling. ✓\n\nBoth statements correctly reflect the implications. **Answer is (c).**",
                "option_a": "I only",
                "option_b": "II only",
                "option_c": "Both I and II",
                "option_d": "Neither I nor II",
                "pyq_year": "2025",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "The World Bank warned that India could become one of the first places where wet-bulb temperatures routinely exceed 35°C. Which of the following statements best reflect(s) the implication of the above-said report?\n\nI. Peninsular India will most likely suffer from flooding, tropical cyclones and droughts.\nII. The survival of animals including humans will be affected as shedding of their body heat through perspiration becomes difficult.\n\nSelect the correct answer using the code given below.",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "The wet-bulb temperature is a measure that combines heat AND humidity. \n\nAt a wet-bulb temperature of **35°C**, the human body can no longer cool itself through sweat evaporation because the air is too saturated with moisture — this makes it potentially lethal for humans and animals.\n\n(I) 'Peninsular India will suffer from flooding, tropical cyclones and droughts' — **CORRECT**. \n\nThe same warming patterns that cause extreme wet-bulb temperatures are associated with broader climate disruptions in peninsular India: \n\n- intensified monsoons leading to flooding, \n- warmer sea surface temperatures fueling more powerful cyclones, \n- and irregular rainfall patterns causing droughts in some regions. \n\nThese are all documented implications of the warming trend that the **World Bank** report highlights. ✓\n\n(II) 'Survival of animals including humans affected as perspiration becomes difficult' — **CORRECT**. \n\nThis is the core danger of wet-bulb temperatures exceeding **35°C**. \n\nAt this threshold, even a healthy person resting in shade with unlimited water will die within hours because sweat cannot evaporate effectively when humidity is too high. \n\nThe body continues to produce heat through metabolism but cannot shed it, leading to hyperthermia and organ failure. \n\nThis affects all mammals and many other animals that rely on evaporative cooling. ✓\n\nBoth statements correctly reflect the implications. **Answer is (c).**"

Its crystal structure closely resembles that of ice, which makes it an excellent ice nucleating agent. When dispersed into clouds, silver iodide particles cause supercooled water droplets to freeze and grow into precipitation.\n- Potassium iodide (KI) — Often used alongside silver iodide to enhance the seeding effect.\n\nOther options are incorrect:\n- Silver nitrate (AgNO₃) — A different silver compound, not the standard cloud-seeding agent.\n- Potassium nitrate (KNO₃) — Used as fertilizer and in gunpowder, not for cloud seeding.\n- Potassium chloride (KCl) — Used as fertilizer and salt substitute, not typically for cloud seeding.\n\nIndia has conducted cloud seeding experiments, including Project Varshadhare in Karnataka, using silver iodide. **Answer is (a).**",

                "question": "With reference to the planet Earth, consider the following statements:\n\nI. Rain forests produce more oxygen than that produced by oceans.\nII. Marine phytoplankton and photosynthetic bacteria produce about 50% of world's oxygen.\nIII. Well-oxygenated surface water contains several folds higher oxygen than that in atmospheric air.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "B",
                "answer_explanation": "(I) 'Rainforests produce more oxygen than oceans' — **INCORRECT**. This is a common misconception. Oceans produce more oxygen than all land-based forests combined. Marine phytoplankton (tiny floating plants/algae) and photosynthetic cyanobacteria in the oceans are responsible for 50-80% of Earth's oxygen production. Tropical rainforests, while important, also consume much of the oxygen they produce through respiration and decomposition. ✗\n\n(II) 'Marine phytoplankton and photosynthetic bacteria produce about 50% of world's oxygen' — **CORRECT**. This is the key fact. Phytoplankton like diatoms and cyanobacteria like Prochlorococcus (the most abundant photosynthetic organism on Earth) carry out photosynthesis in the ocean surface waters and produce roughly 50% or more of global oxygen. ✓\n\n(III) 'Well-oxygenated surface water contains several folds higher oxygen than atmospheric air' — **INCORRECT**. It's actually the opposite! Atmospheric air contains about 21% oxygen (210,000 ppm), while even well-oxygenated surface water contains only about 6-8 mg/L of dissolved oxygen. Water holds far LESS oxygen than air because gases dissolve poorly in water compared to their concentration in the atmosphere. ✗\n\nOnly Statement II is correct. **Answer is (b).**",
                "option_a": "I and II",
                "option_b": "II only",

                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "The Nature Restoration Law (NRL) was enacted by the European Union (EU). It was adopted in June 2024 after prolonged negotiations and became the first comprehensive, continent-wide law specifically aimed at restoring degraded ecosystems.\n\nKey features of the NRL: It requires EU member states to put in place restoration measures covering at least 20% of the EU's land and sea areas by 2030, and all ecosystems in need of restoration by 2050. It covers terrestrial, freshwater, marine, and urban ecosystems including forests, wetlands, rivers, grasslands, and agricultural lands.\n\nThe World Bank (option b) and OECD (option c) are international organizations, not legislative bodies — they cannot 'enact' laws. The FAO (option d) provides guidance and technical assistance but also cannot enact binding legislation. Only the EU, as a supranational body with legislative powers, could enact such a law binding on its member states. **Answer is (a).**",

In aviation, it can be a source of carbon for combining with hydrogen to create synthetic low-carbon fuel.\n\nSelect the correct answer using the code given below.",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "Direct Air Capture (DAC) is a technology that literally pulls CO₂ directly out of the ambient air using chemical filters or solvents. Once captured, this CO₂ can be used in several ways:\n\n(I) Carbon sequestration — **YES**. The captured CO₂ can be permanently stored underground in geological formations (like depleted oil wells or saline aquifers). This is the most straightforward climate benefit — removing CO₂ from the atmosphere and locking it away. ✓\n\n(II) Plastic production and food processing — **YES**. Captured CO₂ is a carbon source that can be used as a feedstock for making plastics (carbon-based polymers). In food processing, CO₂ is used for carbonation of beverages, as a preservative in food packaging (modified atmosphere packaging), and in processes like decaffeination. ✓\n\n(III) Synthetic aviation fuel — **YES**. Captured CO₂ can be combined with green hydrogen (produced from renewable energy) to create synthetic kerosene — a low-carbon jet fuel. Aviation is one of the hardest sectors to decarbonize, and synthetic fuels from DAC are seen as a promising solution. Companies like Climeworks and Carbon Engineering are already working on this. ✓\n\nAll three statements are correct. **Answer is (c).**",

                "answer": "B",
                "answer_explanation": "Coal gasification is a process where coal is converted into a mixture of gases called 'syngas' (synthesis gas), which primarily contains carbon monoxide (CO) and hydrogen (H₂). This syngas can then be used as a building block for various chemical products.\n\n(I) Ethanol — **YES**. Syngas (CO + H₂) can be converted into ethanol through catalytic processes (like Fischer-Tropsch synthesis or fermentation using special bacteria). This is a well-established pathway. ✓\n\n(II) Nitroglycerine — **NO**. Nitroglycerine is made through nitration chemistry — specifically, by treating glycerol with a mixture of concentrated nitric acid and sulfuric acid. This is a completely different chemical process that has nothing to do with coal gasification or syngas. ✗\n\n(III) Urea — **YES**. Syngas provides hydrogen, which can be combined with nitrogen (from air) to produce ammonia (NH₃) through the Haber process. This ammonia is then reacted with CO₂ to produce urea — one of the most widely used fertilizers. India's coal gasification-based fertilizer plants (like the Talcher plant in Odisha) use exactly this process. ✓\n\nSo coal gasification can help produce ethanol and urea, but NOT nitroglycerine. **Answer is (b) Only two.**",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Coal gasification is a process where coal is converted into a mixture of gases called *syngas* (synthesis gas), which primarily contains *carbon monoxide* (CO) and *hydrogen* (H₂). \n\nThis syngas can then be used as a building block for various chemical products.\n\n(I) Ethanol — **YES**. \n\nSyngas (CO + H₂) can be converted into ethanol through catalytic processes (like *Fischer-Tropsch synthesis* or fermentation using special bacteria). \n\nThis is a well-established pathway. ✓\n\n(II) Nitroglycerine — **NO**. \n\nNitroglycerine is made through nitration chemistry — specifically, by treating glycerol with a mixture of concentrated *nitric acid* and *sulfuric acid*. \n\nThis is a completely different chemical process that has nothing to do with coal gasification or syngas. ✗\n\n(III) Urea — **YES**. \n\nSyngas provides hydrogen, which can be combined with nitrogen (from air) to produce *ammonia* (NH₃) through the *Haber process*. \n\nThis ammonia is then reacted with CO₂ to produce *urea* — one of the most widely used fertilizers. \n\n*India's coal gasification-based fertilizer plants* (like the *Talcher plant in Odisha*) use exactly this process. ✓\n\nSo coal gasification can help produce ethanol and urea, but NOT nitroglycerine. \n\n**Answer is (b) Only two.**"

            "single": {
                "question": "Consider the following statements:\n\n**Statement I:** Circular economy reduces the emissions of greenhouse gases.\n\n**Statement II:** Circular economy reduces the use of raw materials as inputs.\n\n**Statement III:** Circular economy reduces wastage in the production process.\n\nWhich one of the following is correct in respect of the above statements?",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "Statement I (Assertion): Circular economy reduces greenhouse gas emissions.\n\nStatement II: Circular economy reduces the use of raw materials as inputs. — **CORRECT**. A circular economy is based on the principles of 'reduce, reuse, recycle, and recover.' Instead of the linear model (take → make → use → dispose), resources are kept in use for as long as possible. This means less virgin raw material extraction (mining, logging, drilling), which directly reduces the energy consumed and emissions generated in extraction, processing, and transportation of new materials. ✓ Explains Statement I.\n\nStatement III: Circular economy reduces wastage in production. — **CORRECT**. By designing products for durability, repairability, and recyclability, and by closing material loops, a circular economy significantly reduces waste. Less waste means less landfill methane emissions, less energy spent on waste management, and less need to produce replacement goods. ✓ Explains Statement I.\n\nBoth mechanisms — reducing raw material use and reducing waste — contribute to lowering greenhouse gas emissions. For example, recycling aluminum uses 95% less energy than producing it from bauxite ore. Both statements are correct and both explain Statement I. **Answer is (a).**",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Statement I (Assertion): **Circular economy reduces greenhouse gas emissions.**\n\nStatement II: **Circular economy reduces the use of raw materials as inputs.** — **CORRECT**. A circular economy is based on the principles of 'reduce, reuse, recycle, and recover.' \n\nInstead of the linear model (take → make → use → dispose), resources are kept in use for as long as possible. \n\nThis means less virgin raw material extraction (mining, logging, drilling), which directly reduces the energy consumed and emissions generated in extraction, processing, and transportation of new materials. \n\n✓ Explains Statement I.\n\nStatement III: **Circular economy reduces wastage in production.** — **CORRECT**. This is called \"**ant-fungus mutualism.**\"\n\n**How it works:**\n\n1. **Leafcutter ants** cut pieces of leaves and carry them to their colony  \n2. They **DON'T** eat the leaves — instead, they use the leaves as **FERTILIZER** to grow fungus  \n3. The fungus garden is their actual food source (fed to larvae)  \n4. The ants carefully maintain these gardens — protecting from pests, removing decayed material, and adding fresh leaves  \n\nThis is one of nature's most fascinating examples of **agriculture** by non-humans! **Leafcutter ants** have been \"farming\" for about **50 million years** — long before humans started agriculture.\n\n**Cockroaches**, **crabs**, and **spiders** are **NOT** known as fungus cultivators."

When a wild animal is declared protected, such animal is entitled for equal protection whether it is found in protected areas or outside.\n3. Apprehension of a protected wild animal becoming a danger to human life is sufficient ground for its capture or killing.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "**The answer is (A) Statements 1 and 2.**\n\n**Statement 1 is CORRECT:** Under the Wildlife Protection Act, 1972, all wild animals that are hunted become government property. The Bombay High Court (2012) confirmed that wild animals, including tigers, are \"government property for all purposes.\"\n\n**Statement 2 is CORRECT:** The law protects wild animals EQUALLY whether they're inside a national park or roaming outside in a village. A tiger has the same legal protection in a forest reserve as it does in someone's farmland.\n\n**Statement 3 is WRONG:** The law does NOT allow killing/capturing a wild animal just because someone FEARS it might become dangerous. There must be an ACTUAL danger — the animal must have actually become a danger to human life, or be diseased/disabled beyond recovery. Only then can the Chief Wildlife Warden authorize action.\n\n**Key difference:** \"Apprehension of danger\" (just fear) ≠ \"Actual danger.\" Only actual danger justifies action.",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "**The answer is (A) Statements 1 and 2.**\n\n**Statement 1 is CORRECT:** \n\nUnder the *Wildlife Protection Act, 1972*, all wild animals that are hunted become *government property*. The *Bombay High Court (2012)* confirmed that wild animals, including tigers, are \"government property for all purposes.\"\n\n**Statement 2 is CORRECT:** \n\nThe law protects wild animals *EQUALLY* whether they're inside a national park or roaming outside in a village. A tiger has the same legal protection in a forest reserve as it does in someone's farmland.\n\n**Statement 3 is WRONG:** \n\nThe law does *NOT* allow killing/capturing a wild animal just because someone *FEARS* it might become dangerous. There must be an *ACTUAL danger* — the animal must have actually become a danger to human life, or be diseased/disabled beyond recovery. Only then can the *Chief Wildlife Warden* authorize action.\n\n**Key difference:** \n\n\"Apprehension of danger\" (just fear) ≠ \"Actual danger.\" Only actual danger justifies action."

                "answer": "C",

Most of Earth's phosphorus is locked in rocks and sedimentary deposits. Over time, weathering and erosion release phosphate ions into soils and water. Plants absorb phosphorus, animals eat plants, and when organisms die, phosphorus returns to the soil and eventually to sediments. Carbon cycle's main source is atmospheric CO2. Nitrogen cycle's main source is atmospheric N2. Sulphur cycle involves volcanic emissions.\n\nOnly the phosphorus cycle depends primarily on rock weathering.\n\nAnswer: (c).",

                "question": "If a particular plant species is placed under Schedule VI of The Wildlife Protection Act, 1972, what is the implication?",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "Schedule VI of the Wildlife Protection Act, 1972 deals with \"specified plants\" — plants that need special protection.\n\nIf a plant is listed under Schedule VI, it means a person CANNOT freely cultivate it. They need a LICENSE from the Chief Wildlife Warden or an authorized officer of the State Government. The license specifies the area where cultivation is allowed and any conditions to follow.\n\nSo it's not a complete ban — cultivation is allowed, but only with proper permission and under regulated conditions.\n\n**Why not the others?**\n- B: It's not a total ban on cultivation. You CAN grow it — with a license.\n- C: Schedule VI has nothing to do with genetically modified crops.\n- D: Schedule VI plants are protected species, not invasive or harmful ones.\n\n**Answer: A (A license is required to cultivate that plant).**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** Schedule VI plants = need a LICENSE to cultivate = regulated, not banned. Don't confuse with invasive species or GM crops.",
                "option_a": "a license is required to cultivate that plant.",
                "option_b": "Such a plant cannot be cultivated under any circumstances.",
                "option_c": "It is a Genetically Modified crop plant.",
                "option_d": "Such a plant is invasive and harmful to the ecosystem.",
                "pyq_year": "2020",
                "marks": 4,
                "negative_marks": 1,
                "formatted_question": "If a particular plant species is placed under Schedule VI of The Wildlife Protection Act, 1972, what is the implication?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Schedule VI of the *Wildlife Protection Act, 1972* deals with *\"specified plants\"* — plants that need special protection.\n\nIf a plant is listed under Schedule VI, it means a person *CANNOT* freely cultivate it. \n\nThey need a *LICENSE* from the *Chief Wildlife Warden* or an authorized officer of the *State Government*. \n\nThe license specifies the area where cultivation is allowed and any conditions to follow.\n\nSo it's not a complete ban — cultivation is allowed, but only with proper permission and under regulated conditions.\n\n**Why not the others?**\n- B: It's not a total ban on cultivation. You *CAN* grow it — with a license.\n- C: Schedule VI has nothing to do with genetically modified crops.\n- D: Schedule VI plants are protected species, not invasive or harmful ones.\n\n**Answer: A (A license is required to cultivate that plant).**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** Schedule VI plants = need a *LICENSE* to cultivate = regulated, not banned. Don't confuse with invasive species or *GM crops*."

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "Steel slag is a byproduct from steel manufacturing. It's very versatile and can be used in multiple ways:\n\n1. Construction of base road — **CORRECT**: Steel slag is used as an aggregate in asphalt for road construction. Asphalt (used for roads) is made of aggregates (like steel/iron slag, sand, gravel) bound together with bitumen. Steel slag provides strength and durability to road surfaces.\n\n2. Improvement of agricultural soil — **CORRECT**: Steel slag can correct soil acidity (useful for acidic soils), provide nutrients to plants, and serve as a silicate fertilizer that supplies silicon to crops. It improves overall soil health.\n\n3. Production of cement — **CORRECT**: Steel slag can be used as a raw material in cement manufacturing — up to 10% in cement clinker production. It can also replace granulated blast furnace slag (up to 10%) in making Portland Slag Cement.\n\nAll three uses are correct. **Answer: D.**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** Steel slag = road construction + soil improvement + cement production. It's a waste product that has valuable applications. Think of it as \"waste to wealth.\" ",

                "option_b": "2 and 3 only",
                "option_c": "1 and 3 only",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Steel slag is a byproduct from steel manufacturing. \n\nIt's very versatile and can be used in multiple ways:\n\n1. While the population density inside the park is low (4-5 persons/km²), there ARE 73 villages and settlements (called \"Dhanis\") inside the park.                 "answer_explanation": "**Statement 1** (Female leads elephant group) — **CORRECT**: In Indian/Asian elephant herds, the oldest female is always the leader (matriarch). Elephant herds are led by experienced females, not males. Males typically leave the herd when they mature.\n\n**Statement 2** (Maximum gestation can be 22 months) — **CORRECT**: Elephants have the longest pregnancy of any land animal — about 640-660 days (roughly 21-22 months or 95 weeks).\n\n**Statement 3** (Can go on calving till 40 years only) — **NOT CORRECT**: Female elephants live for 60-70 years and can continue reproducing much beyond 40 years. While fertility decreases after age 50, many elephants continue to have calves until age 65 or so. They have about 4 offspring in their lifetime. So saying \"only till 40\" is wrong.\n\n**Statement 4** (Kerala has highest elephant population) — **NOT CORRECT**: Karnataka has the highest number of wild elephants among southern states (6,049), followed by Kerala. Overall, South India leads with 14,612 elephants.\n\n**Answer: A (1 and 2 only).**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** Elephant facts: female-led herds + 22-month pregnancy (longest of any mammal) + can reproduce beyond 40 (up to 65!) + Karnataka has most elephants in south (not Kerala).",

Additionally, biochar reduces N2O (nitrous oxide) emissions from soil and increases overall nitrogen retention and efficiency.\n\n**Statement 3** (Helps retain water for longer) — **CORRECT**: Biochar is highly porous (full of tiny holes), like a sponge.                 "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "The Social Cost of Carbon (SCC) is a way to put a DOLLAR VALUE on the damage caused by emitting greenhouse gases.\n\nSimply put: if you release one extra tonne of CO2 into the atmosphere today, the SCC estimates how much economic damage that will cause over time — through things like sea level rise, agricultural losses, health impacts, and extreme weather events.\n\nFor example, India's SCC is estimated at about $86 per tonne of CO2. At current emission levels, India loses roughly $210 billion annually due to climate change damages.\n\nThis number helps policymakers decide whether climate action is worth the investment — if reducing 1 tonne of CO2 costs less than $86, it's economically beneficial to do so.\n\n**Why not the others?**\n- B: This describes fossil fuel consumption, not damage cost.\n- C: This describes climate refugee adaptation costs.\n- D: This describes individual carbon footprint, not the economic damage.\n\n**Answer: A.**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** Social Cost of Carbon = dollar value of damage from 1 tonne of CO2 emissions. India's SCC ≈ $86/tonne. It's a decision-making tool for climate policy.",

At current emission levels, India loses roughly *$210 billion* annually due to climate change damages.\n\nThis number helps policymakers decide whether climate action is worth the investment — if reducing 1 tonne of *CO2* costs less than *$86*, it's economically beneficial to do so.\n\n**Why not the others?**\n- B: This describes fossil fuel consumption, not damage cost.\n- C: This describes climate refugee adaptation costs.\n- D: This describes individual carbon footprint, not the economic damage.\n\n**Answer: A.**\n\n**Key Takeaway:** *Social Cost of Carbon* = dollar value of damage from 1 tonne of *CO2* emissions. *India's SCC* ≈ *$86/tonne*. It's a decision-making tool for climate policy."

When organisms die and decompose, sulphur returns to the soil. So soil is a key component of the sulphur cycle, not separate from it.\n\n3. This is called secondary salinization and is a major problem in irrigated farmlands across India and the world.\n\n> **REMEMBER:** Organic matter INCREASES water retention (Statement 1 is opposite). So when there's MORE CO2 in the atmosphere, plants can potentially grow FASTER and BIGGER — just like giving extra fertilizer to crops.\n\nThink of it this way: CO2 is like food for plants. More food = more growth. This effect is called 'carbon fertilization.'\n\n**Why the other options are wrong:**\n- Option B describes the greenhouse effect (global warming), not carbon fertilization.\n- Option C describes ocean acidification (CO2 dissolving in oceans makes them more acidic), not carbon fertilization.\n- Option D describes climate adaptation, which is a completely different concept.\n\n**Important note: While carbon fertilization sounds positive, it has limits. Beyond a certain point, other factors like water, nutrients, and temperature become limiting. Also, increased CO2 causes global warming, which can harm plants in other ways.**\n\n> **REMEMBER:** Carbon fertilization = MORE CO2 → MORE plant growth (through enhanced photosynthesis). Don't confuse with greenhouse effect (warming) or ocean acidification (acidic oceans).",

It is one of the most important indicators of water pollution in aquatic ecosystems. A high BOD value means there is a lot of organic matter in the water, which in turn means the water is highly polluted — because more oxygen is being consumed by microorganisms to decompose this organic matter, leaving less oxygen for fish and other aquatic life.\n\n**Option (a)** is wrong —** BOD has nothing to do with blood oxygen levels.\n\n**Option (b)** is wrong —** it is not used for forest ecosystems.\n\n**Option (d)** is wrong —** it has nothing to do with high altitude regions.         {
            "question_id": "9621",
            "question_type": "SINGLE",
            "subject_name": "Environment",
            "topic_name": "",
            "subtopic_name": "",
            "source": "",
            "is_pyq": true,
            "category": "PYQ",
            "single": {
                "question": "In the context of mitigating the impending global warming due to anthropogenic emissions of carbon dioxide, which of the following can be potential sites for carbon sequestration?\n\n1. Abandoned and uneconomic coal seams\n2. Depleted oil and gas reservoirs\n3. Subterranean deep saline formations\n\nSelect the correct answer using the code given below:",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "**All three are potential sites for geological carbon sequestration.** Carbon sequestration refers to the process of capturing atmospheric CO2 and storing it long-term to mitigate global warming. Geological sequestration involves injecting captured CO2 deep underground into suitable rock formations where it is trapped and stored permanently. The three main types of geological storage sites are: (1) Abandoned and uneconomic coal seams: CO2 can be injected into coal seams that are too deep or thin to be economically mined. The coal surface adsorbs (binds) CO2 molecules, trapping them. An additional benefit is that injecting CO2 can displace methane trapped in the coal, which can then be recovered and used as fuel — this is called Enhanced Coal Bed Methane (ECBM) recovery.\n\n(2) Depleted oil and gas reservoirs: These are geological formations that previously held oil or gas for millions of years, proving they can trap fluids effectively. Once the oil/gas is extracted, the empty pore spaces can be filled with CO2. Injecting CO2 can also help extract remaining oil — this is called Enhanced Oil Recovery (EOR).\n\n(3) Subterranean deep saline formations: These are deep underground layers of porous rock saturated with brine (salty water). They have the largest potential storage capacity among all geological options. CO2 injected into these formations dissolves in the brine or gets trapped in the rock pores. All three sites are well-established in carbon capture and storage (CCS) science.\n\nSo statements 1, 2, and 3 are all correct.",
                "option_a": "1 and 2 only",
                "option_b": "3 only",
                "option_c": "1 and 3 only",
                "option_d": "1, 2 and 3",
                "pyq_year": "2017",
                "marks": 4,
                "negative_marks": 1,
                "formatted_question": "In the context of mitigating the impending global warming due to anthropogenic emissions of carbon dioxide, which of the following can be potential sites for carbon sequestration?\n\n1. Abandoned and uneconomic coal seams  \n2. Depleted oil and gas reservoirs  \n3. Subterranean deep saline formations  \n\nSelect the correct answer using the code given below:",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "**All three are potential sites for geological carbon sequestration.** \n\nCarbon sequestration refers to the process of capturing atmospheric CO2 and storing it long-term to mitigate global warming. \n\nGeological sequestration involves injecting captured CO2 deep underground into suitable rock formations where it is trapped and stored permanently. \n\nThe three main types of geological storage sites are: \n\n- **(1) Abandoned and uneconomic coal seams:** \n  CO2 can be injected into coal seams that are too deep or thin to be economically mined. \n  The coal surface adsorbs (binds) CO2 molecules, trapping them. \n  An additional benefit is that injecting CO2 can displace methane trapped in the coal, which can then be recovered and used as fuel — this is called **Enhanced Coal Bed Methane (ECBM)** recovery.\n\n- **(2) Depleted oil and gas reservoirs:** \n  These are geological formations that previously held oil or gas for millions of years, proving they can trap fluids effectively. \n  Once the oil/gas is extracted, the empty pore spaces can be filled with CO2. \n  Injecting CO2 can also help extract remaining oil — this is called **Enhanced Oil Recovery (EOR)**.\n\n- **(3) Subterranean deep saline formations:** \n  These are deep underground layers of porous rock saturated with brine (salty water). \n  They have the largest potential storage capacity among all geological options. \n  CO2 injected into these formations dissolves in the brine or gets trapped in the rock pores. \n\nAll three sites are well-established in carbon capture and storage (CCS) science. \n\nSo statements 1, 2, and 3 are all correct."
            }
        },

                "answer_explanation": "**All three practices help in water conservation in agriculture: (1) Reduced or zero tillage: No-till farming (also called zero tillage or direct drilling) is a way of growing crops without disturbing the soil through ploughing.** This increases the amount of water that infiltrates into the soil, increases organic matter retention, and reduces surface evaporation — all of which conserve water.\n\n(2) Applying gypsum before irrigating: Gypsum (calcium sulphate) improves soil structure, especially in sodic soils. It improves the ability of soil to drain properly and not become waterlogged, thus increasing the water-use efficiency of crops. It helps water penetrate deeper into the soil rather than running off the surface.\n\n(3) Allowing crop residue to remain in the field: Crop residue left on the field surface acts as mulch — it covers the soil, reduces direct evaporation of moisture from the soil surface, moderates soil temperature, and helps retain soil moisture for longer periods. Many students doubt statement 2 (gypsum) because gypsum is not commonly associated with water conservation in standard textbooks.                 "formatted_answer_explanation": "**Answer: (b) 2 only**\n\n**Statement 1 (✗ WRONG)**: \n\nTwo errors - the agreement was scheduled for **2020**, not **2017**. \n\nAlso, while **195 countries** adopted it, not all had 'signed' it at that point.\n\n**Statement 2 (✓ CORRECT)**: \n\nThe **Paris Agreement** aims to hold global temperature rise to **WELL BELOW 2 degrees C** above pre-industrial levels, with efforts to limit it to **1.5 degrees C**. \n\nThis is the core goal.\n\n**Statement 3 (✗ WRONG)**: \n\nDeveloped countries committed to **$100 billion** per year (NOT **$1,000 billion**). \n\nThe figure is **10 times** less than stated. \n\nAlso, they didn't explicitly 'acknowledge historical responsibility' in the agreement text as this statement implies.\n\n**Elimination strategy**: \n\nIf you know the **$100 billion** figure (not **$1,000 billion**), Statement 3 is clearly wrong, which eliminates (a), (c), (d). \n\nAnswer must be (b).\n\n**Key Paris Agreement facts**: \n\n- Below **2 degrees C** target \n- **$100 billion/year** \n- Bottom-up approach via **NDCs**."

Forests are home to about 80% of the world's terrestrial biodiversity.\n\n**Statement 2 — Resilience of forest ecosystems (✓ CORRECT)** REDD+ promotes sustainable forest management and enhancement of forest carbon stocks, which strengthens the resilience of forest ecosystems against climate change, droughts, and other threats.\n\n**Statement 3 — Poverty reduction (✓ CORRECT)** REDD+ can generate significant financial flows to developing countries (potentially up to US$30 billion/year). This north-to-south flow of funds can support pro-poor development in forested areas, providing alternative livelihoods for forest-dependent communities.\n\nIn simple terms: REDD+ works by creating financial incentives for developing countries to keep their forests standing rather than cutting them down. This simultaneously:\n• Reduces carbon emissions (climate benefit)\n• Protects wildlife and plants (biodiversity)\n• Strengthens ecosystems (resilience)\n• Generates income for poor communities (poverty reduction)\n\nThe correct answer is (d) \"1, 2 and 3\".",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "**Answer: (d) 1, 2 and 3**\n\nAll three statements about UN-REDD+ Programme are correct.\n\nREDD+ stands for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and forest Degradation, plus conservation, sustainable management of forests, and enhancement of forest carbon stocks.\n\n**Statement 1 — Protection of biodiversity (✓ CORRECT)**  \nBy protecting forests from deforestation and degradation, REDD+ directly helps conserve the rich biodiversity that forests harbor. Forests are home to about **80%** of the world's terrestrial biodiversity.\n\n**Statement 2 — Resilience of forest ecosystems (✓ CORRECT)**  \nREDD+ promotes sustainable forest management and enhancement of forest carbon stocks, which strengthens the resilience of forest ecosystems against climate change, droughts, and other threats.\n\n**Statement 3 — Poverty reduction (✓ CORRECT)**  \nREDD+ can generate significant financial flows to developing countries (potentially up to **US$30 billion/year**). This north-to-south flow of funds can support pro-poor development in forested areas, providing alternative livelihoods for forest-dependent communities.\n\nIn simple terms: REDD+ works by creating financial incentives for developing countries to keep their forests standing rather than cutting them down. This simultaneously:\n\n- Reduces carbon emissions (climate benefit)  \n- Protects wildlife and plants (biodiversity)  \n- Strengthens ecosystems (resilience)  \n- Generates income for poor communities (poverty reduction)  \n\nThe correct answer is (d) \"1, 2 and 3\"."

                "question": "Which one of the following is the best description of the term 'ecosystem'?",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "As per NCERT Class 12 Biology (pg 241): An ecosystem includes all LIVING organisms (plants, animals, microorganisms — called biotic components) interacting with EACH OTHER and also with their NON-LIVING physical environment (weather, soil, water, air, sunlight — called abiotic components). The key word is 'together with the environment' — an ecosystem is the combination of BOTH biotic and abiotic components functioning as a unit. Why are the other options insufficient?\n\n(a) 'A community of organisms interacting with one another' — this defines a BIOTIC COMMUNITY, not an ecosystem. A community only includes living organisms and their interactions (like predation, competition, symbiosis). It misses the physical environment (abiotic factors).\n\n(b) 'That part of the Earth inhabited by living organisms' — this defines the BIOSPHERE, which is the sum of all ecosystems on Earth. The biosphere is much larger in scope than a single ecosystem.\n\n(d) 'The flora and fauna of a geographical area' — this is simply a description of BIODIVERSITY in an area. It lists living things but ignores their interactions with each other and with the environment.\n\nThe best answer is (c) because it captures both components: living community + physical environment = ecosystem.\n\nThink of it as: Community (biotic) + Habitat (abiotic) = Ecosystem.\n\nExamples: a pond ecosystem includes fish, algae, insects (biotic) + water, temperature, dissolved oxygen, mud (abiotic).",
                "option_a": "A community of organisms interacting with one another",
                "option_b": "That part of the Earth which is inhabited by living organisms",
                "option_c": "A community of organisms together with the environment in which they live",
                "option_d": "The flora and fauna of a geographical area",
                "pyq_year": "2015",

                "formatted_question": "Which one of the following is the best description of the term 'ecosystem'?",

A community only includes living organisms and their interactions (like predation, competition, symbiosis). It misses the physical environment (*abiotic factors*).\n\n(b) 'That part of the Earth inhabited by living organisms' — this defines the *BIOSPHERE*, which is the sum of all ecosystems on Earth. The GEAC functions under the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change. Completely unrelated.\n\n(d) Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972 — this deals with protecting wildlife and their habitats, not genetic engineering.\n\n**Answer: Environment (Protection) Act, 1986**.\n\n**Key fact:** All biotech/GMO regulatory bodies in India derive their authority from the EPA, 1986.",

                "answer_explanation": "The Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) is a World Bank-managed initiative. Let us check each statement:\n\nStatement 1 is **CORRECT**: FCPF is indeed a global partnership that brings together governments (both donor and developing countries), businesses, civil society organizations, and indigenous peoples. Its four strategic objectives include reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, conserving and enhancing forest carbon stocks, and sustainably managing forests in developing countries. Indigenous peoples are specifically included because forests are often home to indigenous communities whose rights and livelihoods must be respected.\n\nStatement 2 is **INCORRECT**: FCPF does NOT provide financial aid to universities, individual scientists, or research institutions for developing eco-friendly technologies. This statement describes a research funding body, which the FCPF is not. The FCPF works at the COUNTRY level, helping developing nations build their capacity and readiness for REDD+ programmes. It provides grants to countries, not to individual researchers or universities.\n\nStatement 3 is **CORRECT**: The core purpose of FCPF is to assist developing countries in their REDD+ (Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation, plus conservation, sustainable management, and enhancement of forest carbon stocks) efforts.                 "question": "What can be the impact of excessive/inappropriate use of nitrogenous fertilizers in agriculture?\n\n1. Proliferation of nitrogen-fixing microorganisms in soil can occur.\n2. Increase in the acidity of soil can take place.\n3. Leaching of nitrate to the ground water can occur.\n\nSelect the correct answer using the codes given below.",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "Statement 1 is incorrect: When excess nitrogen fertilizer is added to soil, the soil already has plenty of nitrogen available in the form of NH4+ and NO3- ions. Nitrogen-fixing bacteria (like Rhizobium) have a symbiotic relationship with plants — they fix atmospheric nitrogen in exchange for food from the plant. But when nitrogen is already abundantly available from fertilizers, plants have no incentive to maintain this symbiotic association, so nitrogen-fixing microorganisms actually decline rather than proliferate.\n\nStatement 2 is correct: Nitrogenous fertilizers, especially ammonium-based ones, undergo a process called nitrification in soil. During this process, hydrogen ions (H+) are released, which increases soil acidity (lowers pH). This is a well-known environmental impact of long-term chemical fertilizer use.\n\nStatement 3 is correct: Nitrate (NO3-) is highly soluble in water and does not bind well to soil particles. When excess nitrogen fertilizer is applied, the nitrate ions that are not absorbed by plants get washed downward through the soil by rainwater — a process called leaching — and contaminate groundwater. This is a major environmental and health concern (it can cause diseases like 'blue baby syndrome'). So the answer is 2 and 3 only.",

Leaching of nitrate to the ground water can occur.  \n\nSelect the correct answer using the codes given below.",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Statement 1 is incorrect: \n\nWhen excess nitrogen fertilizer is added to soil, the soil already has plenty of nitrogen available in the form of *NH4+* and *NO3-* ions. \n\n*Nitrogen-fixing bacteria* (like *Rhizobium*) have a symbiotic relationship with plants — they fix atmospheric nitrogen in exchange for food from the plant. \n\nBut when nitrogen is already abundantly available from fertilizers, plants have no incentive to maintain this symbiotic association, so nitrogen-fixing microorganisms actually decline rather than proliferate.\n\nStatement 2 is correct: \n\n*Nitrogenous fertilizers*, especially *ammonium-based* ones, undergo a process called *nitrification* in soil. \n\nDuring this process, *hydrogen ions* (*H+*) are released, which increases soil acidity (lowers pH). \n\nThis is a well-known environmental impact of long-term chemical fertilizer use.\n\nStatement 3 is correct: \n\n*Nitrate* (*NO3-*) is highly soluble in water and does not bind well to soil particles. \n\nWhen excess nitrogen fertilizer is applied, the nitrate ions that are not absorbed by plants get washed downward through the soil by rainwater — a process called *leaching* — and contaminate groundwater. \n\nThis is a major environmental and health concern (it can cause diseases like 'blue baby syndrome'). \n\nSo the answer is 2 and 3 only."

                "answer": "B",
                "answer_explanation": "Statement 1 is incorrect (and this is the trap): The statement has two parts joined by 'and'. While the second part is true (CITES is an international agreement between governments), the first part is false — IUCN is NOT an organ/agency of the United Nations. IUCN was founded in 1948 as an independent international organization. It is unique because its membership includes both governments and civil society organizations, but it is not a UN body like UNEP or UNDP. Since even one part of the statement is wrong, the entire\n\nStatement 1 is incorrect.\n\nStatement 2 is correct: IUCN manages thousands of field projects around the world to better manage natural environments. It conducts research, manages conservation projects on the ground, and brings governments, NGOs, scientists, and businesses together.\n\nStatement 3 is correct: CITES is legally binding on all its member States (called 'Parties'). However, CITES does not replace national laws — each country must adopt its own domestic legislation to implement CITES provisions. So CITES provides the international framework, but enforcement happens through each country's own legal system.\n\n**Answer: 2 and 3 only**.\n\n**Key learning:** In compound statements with 'and', both parts must be correct for the statement to be correct.",

IUCN runs thousands of field projects around the world to better manage natural environments.  \n3. CITES is legally binding on the States that have joined it, but this Convention does not take the place of national laws.  \n\nSelect the correct answer using the codes given below.",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Statement 1 is incorrect (and this is the trap): \n\nThe statement has two parts joined by 'and'. \n\nWhile the second part is true (*CITES* is an international agreement between governments), the first part is false — *IUCN* is NOT an organ/agency of the *United Nations*. \n\n*IUCN* was founded in *1948* as an independent international organization. \n\nIt is unique because its membership includes both governments and civil society organizations, but it is not a *UN* body like *UNEP* or *UNDP*. \n\nSince even one part of the statement is wrong, the entire statement 1 is incorrect.\n\nStatement 2 is correct: \n\n*IUCN* manages thousands of field projects around the world to better manage natural environments. \n\nIt conducts research, manages conservation projects on the ground, and brings governments, *NGOs*, scientists, and businesses together.\n\nStatement 3 is correct: \n\n*CITES* is legally binding on all its member States (called 'Parties'). \n\nHowever, *CITES* does not replace national laws — each country must adopt its own domestic legislation to implement *CITES* provisions. \n\nSo *CITES* provides the international framework, but enforcement happens through each country's own legal system.\n\n**Answer: 2 and 3 only**.\n\n**Key learning:** In compound statements with 'and', both parts must be correct for the statement to be correct."
            }
        },

                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "Statement 1 is **CORRECT**: The Green Climate Fund (GCF) was established to help developing countries with both MITIGATION (reducing greenhouse gas emissions — like shifting to renewable energy) and ADAPTATION (adjusting to the impacts of climate change — like building flood defences, drought-resistant crops). It aims to channel $100 billion per year from developed to developing countries. The GCF is balanced in its funding — half goes to mitigation and half to adaptation, with at least 50% of adaptation funding going to particularly vulnerable countries like Small Island Developing States (SIDS) and Least Developed Countries (LDCs).\n\nStatement 2 is **INCORRECT**: The GCF was NOT founded under UNEP, OECD, ADB and the World Bank. It was established in 2010 at the COP 16 (Cancun) under the UNFCCC (United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change) as a financial mechanism. Its headquarters is in Songdo, South Korea. It is governed by a 24-member Board with equal representation from developed and developing countries.                 "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "The question asks what ADDS CO2 to the atmosphere:\n\n(1) Volcanic action — CORRECT: Volcanoes release massive amounts of CO2 trapped in the Earth's interior.\n\n(2) Respiration — CORRECT: All living organisms (plants, animals, microbes) take in O2 and release CO2 during respiration.\n\n(3) Photosynthesis — WRONG: Photosynthesis does the OPPOSITE — it REMOVES CO2 from the atmosphere and converts it into glucose using sunlight. Plants absorb CO2, not release it.\n\n(4) Decay of organic matter — CORRECT: Decomposition of dead organisms by bacteria and fungi releases CO2 back into the atmosphere. Memory trick: Photosynthesis is the only process that TAKES CO2 OUT. Volcanoes, respiration, decay, and burning fossil fuels all ADD CO2.\n\n(Ref: NCERT Class 11 Geography, Ch.15)",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "The question asks what *ADDS CO2* to the atmosphere:\n\n(1) *Volcanic action* — *CORRECT*: Volcanoes release massive amounts of *CO2* trapped in the Earth's interior.\n\n(2) *Respiration* — *CORRECT*: All living organisms (*plants, animals, microbes*) take in *O2* and release *CO2* during respiration.\n\n(3) *Photosynthesis* — *WRONG*: Photosynthesis does the *OPPOSITE* — it *REMOVES CO2* from the atmosphere and converts it into *glucose* using *sunlight*.                 "answer": "B",
                "answer_explanation": "At 3°C+ warming above pre-industrial levels:\n\n**Statement 1** — CORRECT: The terrestrial biosphere (forests, soils) would shift from being a net carbon SINK (absorbing CO2) to a net carbon SOURCE (releasing CO2) — due to increased wildfires, droughts killing forests, and accelerated decomposition of soil organic matter. This creates a dangerous positive feedback loop.\n\n**Statement 2** — CORRECT: Widespread coral mortality would occur. A Biosphere Reserve has three zones:\n\n(1) Core Zone — strictly protected, no human activity (like a National Park).\n\n(2) Buffer Zone — limited human activity, research, and education allowed.\n\n(3) Transition Zone — outermost area where traditional human activities like farming, settlement, and sustainable resource use continue.                 "question": "Brominated flame retardants are used in many household products like mattresses and upholstery. Why is there some concern about their use ?\n\n1. They are highly resistant to degradation in the environment\n2. They are able to accumulate in humans and animals\n\nSelect the correct answer using the code given below.",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "Both concerns are valid:\n\n(1) Persistent in environment — CORRECT: Brominated flame retardants (BFRs) are chemically very stable, meaning they resist natural breakdown (biodegradation). They persist in soil, water, and sediments for years — similar to how DDT and PCBs persist.\n\n(2) Bioaccumulation — CORRECT: Because BFRs are fat-soluble (lipophilic), they accumulate in the fatty tissues of animals and humans over time. They biomagnify up the food chain — higher predators (including humans) have higher concentrations. BFRs have been found in human breast milk and blood. These two properties together — persistence + bioaccumulation — are exactly what makes Persistent Organic Pollutants (POPs) dangerous. BFRs are regulated under the Stockholm Convention on POPs.",

They are highly resistant to degradation in the environment  \n2. They are able to accumulate in humans and animals  \n\nSelect the correct answer using the code given below.",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Both concerns are valid:\n\n(1) **Persistent in environment** — **CORRECT**: **Brominated flame retardants (BFRs)** are chemically very stable, meaning they resist natural breakdown (**biodegradation**). They persist in soil, water, and sediments for years — similar to how **DDT** and **PCBs** persist.\n\n(2) **Bioaccumulation** — **CORRECT**: Because **BFRs** are fat-soluble (**lipophilic**), they accumulate in the fatty tissues of animals and humans over time. They biomagnify up the food chain — higher predators (including humans) have higher concentrations. **BFRs** have been found in human breast milk and blood. \n\nThese two properties together — **persistence** + **bioaccumulation** — are exactly what makes **Persistent Organic Pollutants (POPs)** dangerous. **BFRs** are regulated under the **Stockholm Convention on POPs**."

Crustaceans (like krill, copepods) are tiny animals that feed on diatoms (primary consumers). Herrings are small fish that eat crustaceans (secondary consumers).                 "formatted_answer_explanation": "A food chain always flows: \n\n*Producer → Primary Consumer → Secondary Consumer.* \n\n*Diatoms* are microscopic algae (*producers — they photosynthesise*). \n\n*Crustaceans* (like *krill, copepods*) are tiny animals that feed on diatoms (*primary consumers*). \n\n*Herrings* are small fish that eat crustaceans (*secondary consumers*). \n\nSo the correct chain is: \n\n*Diatoms → Crustaceans → Herrings.* \n\nAny sequence that puts consumers before producers is wrong. \n\nThis is an *aquatic/marine food chain example*. \n\nRemember: food chains *ALWAYS* start with a producer (*plants/algae*)."

                "answer_explanation": "In ecological succession, grasslands would normally be replaced by shrubs and eventually trees (climax community). But in many grassland ecosystems, this succession is arrested — trees never fully take over. The two main reasons are:\n\n(1) Water limits — grasslands typically occur in regions where rainfall is insufficient to support dense tree growth. Grasses can survive with less water than most trees.\n\n(2) Fire — periodic fires (natural or human-caused) destroy tree seedlings and saplings but grasses quickly regenerate from their root systems. Grasses also intercept most of the rainwater before it reaches the roots of shrub seedlings, limiting their growth. For example, the ecological niche of a sunflower includes: absorbing light, water, and nutrients (for photosynthesis), providing shelter and food for other organisms (bees, ants, birds), and releasing oxygen into the atmosphere.\n\nLet's eliminate the other options:\n\n(a) Ecotone — a transition zone between two ecosystems (e.g., the area between a forest and grassland). It describes a place, not a functional role.\n\n(c) Habitat — describes ONLY where an organism lives (its physical address), NOT what it does. For example, a pond is a habitat for a frog, but the frog's niche includes what it eats, what eats it, when it's active, etc.\n\n(d) Home range — the area over which an animal normally travels in search of food. It's about spatial extent of movement, not functional role.\n\nOnly 'ecological niche' combines physical space + functional role.",

                "answer_explanation": "Only Nostoc (2) is used as a biofertilizer. Biofertilizers are microbial preparations that enhance nutrient uptake by plants from the soil. There are two main types of organisms used as bioinoculants:\n\n(a) Symbiotic nitrogen-fixers: Rhizobium, Bradyrhizobium, Nostoc, Anabaena, etc.\n\n(b) Non-symbiotic/free-living nitrogen-fixers: Azotobacter, Azospirillum, Beijerinckia, etc.\n\nNostoc is a genus of cyanobacteria (blue-green algae) that can fix atmospheric nitrogen into a form usable by plants. It is widely used as a biofertilizer in rice paddies (paddy fields).\n\nAgaricus (1) — **NOT** a biofertilizer. Agaricus is a genus of mushrooms (includes both edible species like button mushroom and poisonous species). While mushrooms decompose organic matter, they are not classified as biofertilizers.\n\nSpirogyra (3) — **NOT** a biofertilizer.                 "question": "With reference to food chains in ecosystems, consider the following statements:\n\n1. A food chains illustrates the order in which a chain of organisms feed upon each other.\n2. Food chains are found within the populations of a species.\n3. A food chain illustrates the numbers of each organism which are eaten by others.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "**Statement 1 is correct** — a food chain is the sequence of 'who eats whom' in a biological community (ecosystem) to obtain nutrition. For example: Grass → Grasshopper → Frog → Snake → Eagle. It shows the ORDER of feeding relationships.\n\n**Statement 2 is wrong** — food chains are NOT found within a single species' population. A food chain by definition involves MULTIPLE species at different trophic levels (producers, primary consumers, secondary consumers, etc.). Feeding relationships within one species' population would be cannibalism, not a food chain.\n\n**Statement 3 is wrong** — a food chain does NOT illustrate the NUMBERS of each organism eaten. It only shows the SEQUENCE/ORDER of feeding, not quantities. The concept of numbers at each level is shown by an 'Ecological Pyramid of Numbers', which is a different concept.\n\nSo only statement 1 is correct.",
                "option_a": "1 only",
                "option_b": "1 and 2 only",
                "option_c": "1, 2 and 3",
                "option_d": "None",
                "pyq_year": "2013",

                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "With reference to food chains in ecosystems, consider the following statements:\n\n1. A food chains illustrates the order in which a chain of organisms feed upon each other.  \n2. Food chains are found within the populations of a species.  \n3. A food chain illustrates the numbers of each organism which are eaten by others.  \n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "**Statement 1 is correct** — a food chain is the sequence of 'who eats whom' in a biological community (ecosystem) to obtain nutrition. \n\nFor example:  \nGrass → Grasshopper → Frog → Snake → Eagle.  \n\nIt shows the *ORDER* of feeding relationships.\n\n**Statement 2 is wrong** — food chains are *NOT* found within a single species' population. \n\nA food chain by definition involves *MULTIPLE species* at different trophic levels (producers, primary consumers, secondary consumers, etc.). \n\nFeeding relationships within one species' population would be *cannibalism*, not a food chain.\n\n**Statement 3 is wrong** — a food chain does *NOT* illustrate the *NUMBERS* of each organism eaten. \n\nIt only shows the *SEQUENCE/ORDER* of feeding, not quantities. \n\nThe concept of numbers at each level is shown by an 'Ecological Pyramid of Numbers', which is a different concept.\n\nSo only statement 1 is correct."

                "question": "Consider the following agricultural practices:\n\n1. Contour bunding\n2. Relay cropping\n3. Zero tillage\n\nIn the context of global climate change, which of the above helps/help in carbon sequestration/storage in the soil?",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "All three agricultural practices help in carbon sequestration in soil: Contour bunding (statement 1) — reduces soil erosion, retaining organic matter and carbon in the soil. By preventing topsoil from washing away, carbon stored in the soil is preserved.\nRelay cropping (statement 2) — ensures continuous plant cover on the soil. Growing a second crop before the first is harvested keeps roots in the ground longer, adding organic matter and keeping carbon locked in the soil.\nZero tillage (statement 3) — by not ploughing/tilling the soil, carbon-rich organic matter is not disturbed and remains stored. Tillage exposes soil carbon to air, converting it to CO₂. All three reduce carbon release and/or increase carbon storage in soil.\n\nAnswer: 1, 2 and 3.",
                "option_a": "1 and 2 only",
                "option_b": "3 only",
                "option_c": "1, 2 and 3",
                "option_d": "None of them",
                "pyq_year": "2012",
                "marks": 4,
                "negative_marks": 1,
                "formatted_question": "Consider the following agricultural practices:\n\n1. Contour bunding  \n2. Relay cropping  \n3. Zero tillage  \n\nIn the context of global climate change, which of the above helps/help in carbon sequestration/storage in the soil?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "All three agricultural practices help in *carbon sequestration* in soil: \n\n*Contour bunding* (statement 1) — reduces soil erosion, retaining organic matter and carbon in the soil. \n\nBy preventing topsoil from washing away, carbon stored in the soil is preserved. \n\n*Relay cropping* (statement 2) — ensures continuous plant cover on the soil. \n\nGrowing a second crop before the first is harvested keeps roots in the ground longer, adding organic matter and keeping carbon locked in the soil. \n\n*Zero tillage* (statement 3) — by not ploughing/tilling the soil, carbon-rich organic matter is not disturbed and remains stored. \n\nTillage exposes soil carbon to air, converting it to CO₂. \n\nAll three reduce carbon release and/or increase carbon storage in soil. \n\nAnswer: 1, 2 and 3."
            }

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "ALL THREE are emissions from coal combustion at thermal power plants.\n\n(1) Carbon dioxide (CO₂): Coal is primarily carbon — burning it produces massive CO₂ (the main greenhouse gas driving climate change).\n\n(2) Oxides of Nitrogen (NOₓ): Formed when nitrogen in coal AND nitrogen in air react at high combustion temperatures. NOₓ causes acid rain and smog.\n\n(3) Oxides of Sulphur (SOₓ): Coal contains sulphur impurities. Burning coal oxidizes this sulphur → SO₂ and SO₃, which cause acid rain and respiratory problems. Many students wrongly think coal only produces CO₂.\n\nIn reality, coal combustion produces a cocktail of pollutants: CO₂ + NOₓ + SOₓ + particulate matter + fly ash + mercury + other heavy metals.                 "formatted_answer_explanation": "ALL THREE are emissions from coal combustion at thermal power plants.\n\n(1) *Carbon dioxide (CO₂)*: Coal is primarily carbon — burning it produces massive *CO₂* (the main greenhouse gas driving climate change).\n\n(2) *Oxides of Nitrogen (NOₓ)*: Formed when nitrogen in coal AND nitrogen in air react at high combustion temperatures. *NOₓ* causes acid rain and smog.\n\n(3) *Oxides of Sulphur (SOₓ)*: Coal contains sulphur impurities.                 "question": "Consider the following statements:\n\n1. Biodiversity is normally greater in the lower latitudes as compared to the higher latitudes.\n2. Along the mountain gradients, biodiversity is normally greater in the lower altitudes as compared to the higher altitudes.\n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "answer": "C",
                "answer_explanation": "Both statements are CORRECT — these are well-established ecological patterns.\n\nStatement 1 — Latitudinal gradient: Biodiversity is HIGHEST near the equator (low latitudes) and DECREASES toward the poles (high latitudes). Reasons: More solar energy, warmer temperatures, higher rainfall, longer evolutionary time, more stable climate → more species. Example: Amazon rainforest has more species than all of Europe combined.\n\nStatement 2 — Altitudinal gradient: As you go UP a mountain, biodiversity DECREASES. Reasons: Temperature drops (~6.5°C per 1000m), oxygen decreases, harsh winds, less soil — conditions become hostile. Mountain base = tropical forest (rich). Mountain top = bare rock/snow (poor). Both gradients follow the same principle: HARSHER CONDITIONS = FEWER SPECIES. Whether you go toward the poles or up a mountain, you're moving toward colder, harsher environments.",
                "option_a": "1 only",
                "option_b": "2 only",

                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "Consider the following statements:\n\n1. Biodiversity is normally greater in the lower latitudes as compared to the higher latitudes.  \n2. Along the mountain gradients, biodiversity is normally greater in the lower altitudes as compared to the higher altitudes.  \n\nWhich of the statements given above is/are correct?",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "Both statements are **CORRECT** — these are well-established ecological patterns.\n\nStatement 1 — **Latitudinal gradient**: \n\nBiodiversity is **HIGHEST** near the **equator** (low latitudes) and **DECREASES** toward the **poles** (high latitudes). \n\nReasons: \n- More solar energy \n- Warmer temperatures \n- Higher rainfall \n- Longer evolutionary time \n- More stable climate → more species. \n\nExample: **Amazon rainforest** has more species than all of **Europe** combined.\n\nStatement 2 — **Altitudinal gradient**: \n\nAs you go **UP** a mountain, biodiversity **DECREASES**. \n\nReasons: \n- Temperature drops (~6.5°C per 1000m) \n- Oxygen decreases \n- Harsh winds \n- Less soil — conditions become hostile. \n\nMountain base = **tropical forest** (rich). \n\nMountain top = **bare rock/snow** (poor). \n\nBoth gradients follow the same principle: **HARSHER CONDITIONS = FEWER SPECIES**. \n\nWhether you go toward the **poles** or up a **mountain**, you're moving toward colder, harsher environments."

                "question": "If a tropical rain forest is removed, it does not regenerate quickly as compared to a tropical deciduous forest. This is because",
                "answer": "A",
                "answer_explanation": "The answer is (a) — tropical rainforest soil is DEFICIENT IN NUTRIENTS. This seems counterintuitive — how can such lush forests have poor soil?\n\nHere's the explanation: In a tropical rainforest, most nutrients are stored IN THE LIVING BIOMASS (trees, plants, animals), NOT in the soil. The nutrient cycle is incredibly fast: leaves fall → decompose quickly in hot, humid conditions → nutrients are immediately reabsorbed by living roots → nothing stays in the soil for long. When you REMOVE the forest: All the nutrients stored in biomass are gone. The thin layer of topsoil gets washed away quickly by heavy tropical rains (leaching). What's left is nutrient-poor, acidic laterite soil. Without nutrients, new plants struggle to grow → slow regeneration.\n\nCompare with TROPICAL DECIDUOUS FOREST: These forests shed leaves seasonally → thick layer of leaf litter accumulates → creates nutrient-rich soil over time. So even after clearing, the soil retains enough nutrients for faster regeneration. Key paradox: Richest forests sit on some of the poorest soils because nutrients are in the TREES, not the GROUND.",
                "option_a": "The soil of rain forest is deficient in nutrients",
                "option_b": "Propagules of the trees in a rain forest have poor viability",
                "option_c": "The rain forest species are slow growing",
                "option_d": "Exotic species invade the fertile soil of rain forest",
                "pyq_year": "2011",
                "marks": 2,
                "negative_marks": 0.66,
                "formatted_question": "If a tropical rain forest is removed, it does not regenerate quickly as compared to a tropical deciduous forest. \n\nThis is because:",
                "formatted_answer_explanation": "The answer is (a) — *tropical rainforest soil is DEFICIENT IN NUTRIENTS*. \n\nThis seems counterintuitive — how can such lush forests have poor soil?\n\nHere's the explanation: \n\nIn a tropical rainforest, most nutrients are stored *IN THE LIVING BIOMASS* (trees, plants, animals), *NOT* in the soil. \n\nThe nutrient cycle is incredibly fast: \n- leaves fall \n- decompose quickly in hot, humid conditions \n- nutrients are immediately reabsorbed by living roots \n- nothing stays in the soil for long. \n\nWhen you *REMOVE* the forest: \n- All the nutrients stored in biomass are gone. \n- The thin layer of topsoil gets washed away quickly by heavy tropical rains (leaching). \n- What's left is nutrient-poor, acidic laterite soil. \n\nWithout nutrients, new plants struggle to grow → slow regeneration.\n\nCompare with *TROPICAL DECIDUOUS FOREST*: \n\nThese forests shed leaves seasonally → thick layer of leaf litter accumulates → creates nutrient-rich soil over time. \n\nSo even after clearing, the soil retains enough nutrients for faster regeneration. \n\nKey paradox: Richest forests sit on some of the poorest soils because nutrients are in the *TREES*, not the *GROUND*."

Discharge of nutrients from the estuaries.\n2. Run-off from the land during the monsoon.\n3. Upwelling in the seas.\n\nSelect the correct answer from the codes given below:",
                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "ALL THREE factors cause harmful algal blooms (HABs) by adding excess nutrients (especially nitrogen and phosphorus) to seawater.\n\n(1) Estuarine discharge: Rivers carry agricultural fertilizers, sewage, and industrial waste into estuaries, which then flow into the sea — nutrient overload.\n\n(2) Monsoon run-off: Heavy rains wash fertilizers and organic matter from farmlands into rivers and eventually into coastal waters — seasonal nutrient surge.\n\n(3) Upwelling: Deep ocean water is naturally rich in nutrients (from decomposed organic matter). When this water rises to the surface, it brings those nutrients up, feeding algal growth.\n\n**The common thread:** All three INCREASE NUTRIENT LEVELS in surface waters → algae get 'overfed' → bloom explosively → some species produce toxins = harmful algal bloom.",

                "formatted_answer_explanation": "ALL THREE factors cause harmful algal blooms (HABs) by adding excess nutrients (especially **nitrogen** and **phosphorus**) to seawater.\n\n(1) **Estuarine discharge**: Rivers carry agricultural fertilizers, sewage, and industrial waste into estuaries, which then flow into the sea — nutrient overload.\n\n(2) **Monsoon run-off**: Heavy rains wash fertilizers and organic matter from farmlands into rivers and eventually into coastal waters — seasonal nutrient surge.\n\n(3) **Upwelling**: Deep ocean water is naturally rich in nutrients (from decomposed organic matter). When this water rises to the surface, it brings those nutrients up, feeding algal growth.\n\n**The common thread:** All three INCREASE NUTRIENT LEVELS in surface waters → algae get 'overfed' → bloom explosively → some species produce toxins = harmful algal bloom."

                "answer": "D",
                "answer_explanation": "ALL FOUR are ways biodiversity supports human existence — answer is (d).\n\n(1) Soil formation (✓): Microorganisms, earthworms, fungi, and plant roots break down rocks and organic matter over centuries to create soil. Without biodiversity, there would be no soil.\n\n(2) Prevention of soil erosion (✓): Plant roots hold soil together, preventing it from being washed or blown away. Grasslands, forests, and mangroves all prevent erosion. Deforestation → erosion → floods.\n\n(3) Recycling of waste (✓): Decomposers (bacteria, fungi, insects) break down dead organisms and waste materials, recycling nutrients back into the ecosystem. Without them, dead matter would pile up endlessly.\n\n(4) Pollination of crops (✓): About 75% of food crops depend on animal pollination — bees, butterflies, birds, bats. Without pollinators, we'd lose most fruits, vegetables, nuts, and seeds. These are called ECOSYSTEM SERVICES — the free 'services' that nature provides to sustain human life.                 "formatted_answer_explanation": "ALL FOUR are ways biodiversity supports human existence — answer is (d).\n\n(1) **Soil formation** (✓): Microorganisms, earthworms, fungi, and plant roots break down rocks and organic matter over centuries to create soil. Without biodiversity, there would be no soil.\n\n(2) **Prevention of soil erosion** (✓): Plant roots hold soil together, preventing it from being washed or blown away. Grasslands, forests, and mangroves all prevent erosion. Deforestation → erosion → floods.\n\n(3) **Recycling of waste** (✓): Decomposers (bacteria, fungi, insects) break down dead organisms and waste materials, recycling nutrients back into the ecosystem. Without them, dead matter would pile up endlessly.\n\n(4) **Pollination of crops** (✓): About **75%** of food crops depend on animal pollination — bees, butterflies, birds, bats.
